Better educated than ever – yet Germany's Gen Z is being shut out of its own future

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Never before have young people faced so many challenges. Young Germans are asking: Will I be better off later in life than my parents are now?

It is becoming increasingly difficult for young people in Germany to find a job after finishing professional training or university. And finding an affordable place to live in a big city and finally starting to live independently is nearly impossible.

Nor do young people feel that politicians are finally taking action on issues that matter to them, such as climate protection. The climate conference in Antalya, Turkey, is a case in point. It therefore shouldn't be a surprise that young people across Europe are taking to the streets this Friday to make their voices heard. In Germany, Gen Z protests took place in 73 cities, coinciding with World Children's Day.

What particularly infuriates young people is being talked about rather than spoken to. Equally infuriating is seeing their protests branded as criminal before they have even begun, all because of the actions of a small minority.

One example from the political realm was when the youth wing of the conservative Christian Democratic Union/Christian Social Union (CDU/CSU) party in Berlin-Mitte suggested deploying the German army against demonstrations.

It can also be seen in the media, such as when Bild, Germany's highest-circulation tabloid, ran the headline: "Young, male, known to the police — who rioted after the demonstrations and where chaos threatens today."

"Young people are taking to the streets because they feel they are not being heard. And, of course, that feeling is strongly reinforced when the press devotes no more than a brief paragraph to their demands while focusing the rest of its coverage on how these protests could become problematic," says Erik Sczygiol, who until recently was deputy secretary-general of the Federal Conference of Student Representatives. "Of course, any potential security concerns need to be addressed, but they should not overshadow media coverage of the protests," he argues.

However, eighteen-year-old Erik Sczygiol is an exception. He was invited to participate in the launch of the World Population Report in Bonn alongside representatives of the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) and the German Foundation for World Population (DSW).This year's report is particularly noteworthy because it focuses on Gen Z. The report is titled "Succeeding Generations: Empowering young people to chart the future."

The United Nations surveyed 109,000 people across 73 countries. Some of the findings are encouraging. Young people worldwide are better educated than any generation before them. Today, 78% of adolescents complete at least upper-secondary education. Yet finding a job is becoming increasingly difficult, while informal employment is on the rise across the globe.

This economic uncertainty has far-reaching consequences for young people. According to the report, financial independence is often not achieved until one's mid-to-late twenties, and a stable partnership and parenthood are often not realities until one's early-to-mid thirties.

Nor do young people have a seat at the political decision-making table when it comes to shaping future reforms in the labor market, housing or climate protection. Florian Brod, 25, points out: "Almost half of the world's population is under 30, yet only 2.6% of parliamentarians worldwide are under 30."

Young people need and want change to protect their livelihoods and those of future generations. "Yet those who will have to live longest with the consequences of today's decisions are precisely the ones who have hardly any representation in the political decision-making process," says Brod.

At least Brod is somewhat closer to the political decision-making process. He has served as a Youth Delegate for Sustainable Development for almost two years. In this role, he represents the interests of young people in Germany at United Nations conferences. This is certainly an important step towards giving his generation a greater say in political processes.

Nevertheless, both Florian Brod and Erik Sczygiol, who represents students, have had similar experiences. They often participate in conferences and panel discussions and are invited to submit their opinions, but these invitations often amount to little more than symbolic gestures.

"Having access to an international conference is a first step. But without substantive briefings ahead of the conference, without involvement in preliminary negotiations and, in some cases, without access to negotiation texts, there is little opportunity for us to help shape the outcome. When young people see their demands making little headway politically while they are simultaneously excluded from the search for solutions, frustration inevitably builds," says Brod.

Sczygiol adds: "We often get the feeling at the Federal Youth Conference that we were invited simply to provide a nice photo opportunity. We may be sitting at the table, but often only once a bill has already gone through 17 different drafts and is just days away from its final vote in the Bundestag. By that stage, young people have virtually no say in changing anything, and their voices are barely heard. Their contributions are often simply dismissed with: 'Well, that's just typical youthful nonsense.' And that is something that really frustrates young people."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Medical student Amélie Belosevic knows that feeling all too well. She served as Germany's Youth Delegate at the World Health Assembly in Geneva this May. Twenty-five countries sent youth representatives, which Belosevic sees as a clear step forward. However, she has one criticism. According to the United Nations Population Fund, 90% of the world's 1.8 billion young people live in the Global South. Yet, the region was represented by only eight delegates. As a result, Africa, Latin America and large parts of Asia were left virtually voiceless.

Her demands for Germany include better job prospects for young people entering the workforce and sustainable pension systems 40 years from now. "For a very long time, people have said, 'I believe my children will have a better life than I do.' But that is no longer necessarily true. So it makes sense then that those who feel the greatest anxiety about the future are the people who have most of their life ahead of them. Sometimes, people don't understand that. We, young people, need to be able to believe in our future again!"

Dakota Johnson and Machine Gun Kelly’s Couple Style Is in the Bag

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dakota Johnson and Machine Gun Kelly have much more in common than their fondness for leather jackets and a little rock-and-roll romance. Very good handbags, for one.

On Thursday evening, the pair were photographed leaving a restaurant in rainy Los Angeles, bundled up against the chill and threats of floods. Johnson wore a long chocolate-brown leather coat over charcoal gray tailored trousers and glossy black loafers, with a voluminous emerald-green scarf wrapped around her neck. Kelly, meanwhile, opted for an oversized black leather jacket layered over a hoodie, wide-leg black pants, extremely chunky shoes, and a black-and-white hand-print knitted beanie.

Johnson carried a generously proportioned, vintage leopard-print Prada bag with black leather straps—a familiar favorite that’s accompanied her on previous outings with Kelly. Kelly, meanwhile, appeared to be sporting a capacious black-and-white Louis Vuitton tote from the house’s 2021 collaboration with Swiss artist Urs Fischer. The collection saw Fischer playfully reinterpret the house’s famous Monogram, recreating its motifs from memory in a hand-drawn pattern, with a velvet-like tufted finish that makes for a tactile bag. It’s an interesting accessories pairing of equally loud and collectible pieces. And both suggest an appreciation for a look where the bag is the outfit.

Dakota Johnson, of course, has long been a serious handbag enthusiast, with a wardrobe that runs from classic Gucci Jackies to studded Valentino shoulder bags. MGK has also been spotted on more than one occasion with a trusty oversized denim Hermès Birkin. Clearly, neither is particularly interested in keeping their accessories understated.

As their couple style continues to develop, the two seem increasingly in sync without dressing identically. There’s a heck of a lot of leather, increasingly oversized proportions, and affections for an aesthetic that’s equal parts offbeat and glam-rock. And who says an It-bag has to be hers alone?

Tom Brady in Talks to Buy Stake in Chelsea After Birmingham Exit

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

According to reports, Tom Brady is in discussions to buy a small stake in a Premier League club. The news comes just weeks after the American football legend sold his minority share in Birmingham City.

Brady is one of the greatest athletes of all time, having become a Super Bowl champion on seven occasions. He is best remembered for his 20 seasons with the New England Patriots, before finishing his career with the Tampa Bay Buccaneers.

Having retired in 2023, the former NFL ​quarterback joined Birmingham's ownership group in August 2023 through a partnership with Knighthead Capital Management. Now with a taste of football investment, Brady is set to claim a small stake in one of the Premier League's biggest clubs.

When the POTUS was called out for cheating, he gave a typical response.

The initial report comes from the Guardian, which stated that Brady is in discussions about buying a stake in Chelsea. What's more, his Birmingham City sale decision was made to pave the way for an investment at Stamford Bridge.

Following the Guardian's report, GIVEMESPORT understands it will be a small stake. The deal is not done yet, but all sides are optimistic. The investment comes down to the relationship between Brady and the managing partner of Clearlake Capital, Behdad Eghbali.

After Todd Boehly and Mark Walter sold their shares in September, Clearlake Capital now owns 86.5% of Chelsea.

Brady was notably a guest of the Premier League side when they won the 2025 Club World Cup in New Jersey. He was then seen celebrating with the players after their 3-0 final win over Paris Saint-Germain.

Brady also owns a minority stake in the NFL team Las Vegas Raiders.

If Brady does invest in Chelsea, it remains to be seen how hands-on he will be. At Birmingham, he was appointed chair of the club’s advisory board and made headlines when he questioned the “work ethic” of the then manager Wayne Rooney.

During the 2025 Amazon Prime documentary Built in Birmingham: Brady & The Blues, Brady said: "I’m a little worried about our head coach’s work ethic."

Business manager Ben Rawitz agreed that Rooney "comes across as lackadaisical." Brady added: “I don’t know, I don’t have great instincts on that.”

Rooney later spoke out to express his disappointment at the exchange, claiming that it "felt unfair". He noted: "I think Tom came in once, which was the day before a game where the days are a little bit lighter anyway, and I don't think he really understood football that well. But what he does understand is, he's a hard worker, we know that."

He then added: "Football is not NFL - NFL works for three months a year. Players do need rest as well, so I think he's very unfair, the way he's come out and portrayed that."

Rooney lasted just 15 matches over 83 days at Birminghambefore being replaced, leaving the team 20th in the Championship.

A statement announcing Brady’s 2026 departure from the club read: “Birmingham City Football Club expresses its sincere gratitude to Tom Brady for his involvement with and unwavering support for the club over the last three years.”

When It's Always All About "Me"

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 20, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

We’re living in the age of “me.” We see most things through the lens of “self”: How’s this experience going for me, and how am I doing? What does this mean for and about me? How’s this person perceiving me? Does my “me” like this or not? Our culture teaches us to be the protagonist of our life, make something of ourselves, be “somebody”—to actualize and optimize ourselves. We should be exceptional, the “star” of our own story, hero of our own journey.

From the moment we can gaze into a mirror, those around us start saying, “That’s you!” “Look at you!” “There you are!” We start being recognized (and celebrated) as a “me,” a “someone” who’s her own unique identity, the one doing this life. A few years later, we learn to advocate for and protect that “me,” snatching toys from other little people, saying “That’s mine,” “Give me my…” We build a sense of possession, of being a “me” who has things or doesn’t.

Soon after (and for the rest of our life), we take on the project of that “me” and start working on behalf of our “me.” We become our own protector, PR rep, agent, and biographer, the narrator of our self-story—the story of “me.”

So too, the message we learn is that this “me” must be moving forward in life, continually progressing towards some better future version of ourselves, a new-and-improved, happier-and-better “me.” We can never rest because we must continue stacking up evidence of our advancement. As we are and where we are, we’re not enough—our “me” still hasn’t done enough. There’s some place, or so we’re taught, at the end of all our progress, where we will have progressed far enough and become enough to make the project of “me” complete, and set it down. Then we’ll be able to experience life directly without our “me” in the way.

We don’t just nurse at the bottle of progress; we also feed on the narrative of materialism, which offers us a system of values and ideas about what “counts” as progress. As the narrative goes, money, success, power, and lots of shiny things will earn us the elusive enough-ness we seek, and the right to stop focusing on our progress and the project of “me.” The problem is that this promised place of enough-ness, where we can finally stop protecting ourselves and progressing on our hero’s journey, never comes. No matter how much we achieve and prove about our worth, enough-ness eludes us, so we must continue pursuing the advancement of our individual “me.”

The self-help market is built around this same premise: that the individual is a project to be continually improved. The message is that our “me” needs to be fixed, or at the very least, needs to be better than she is. We’re supposed to be living more authentically, manifesting the life we deserve, putting ourselves first, distinguishing ourselves, and always becoming our best self (which is definitely not this self).

Self-help teaches us to relentlessly focus on our own experience and how we’re doing in every moment—how our life is going, and, most importantly, how the project of “me” is going. We learn to gauge and evaluate the present moment by whether it gives us what we want and need, whether it’s “good” for us. While paying attention to our own experience and how the present moment is affecting us is profoundly important and necessary for a healthy person, as is the ability to honor one’s own wants and needs, the problem is that this self we’ve learned to place at the center of our attention and indeed existence becomes the operating system through which we experience all of life. We don’t experience life directly, what’s actually happening; we experience what our life says about us, which we also invent and create in our mind.

Does this situation, interaction, or opinion make me feel good about me? Do I feel safe, secure, liked, important as a result of it? Does it support my self-image, the story I tell myself about myself? Am I getting what I need to shore up the version of myself I need to function? We encounter, experience, and react to life through a self-referencing lens, as a reflection of and commentary on who we are.

At the same time, our self-focus is motivated by our most basic instinct: survival. On an evolutionary level, we’re always trying to protect our existence as a species—to stay alive, continue being. Long ago, when we lived in the forest, we had to focus on threats: other animals that might eat us or our offspring, branches that could land on our heads, a dark sky that meant our nest would be flooded. Seeing reality through the lens of how it would impact our survival—through the lens of “me” and mine has deep biological roots.

And indeed, statistically, it made more sense to pay attention to what could kill us than what could delight us, like finding a great berry to eat. This now translates into “negativity bias,” our tendency to focus on what’s negative over what’s positive. But threats in modern times are generally not branches or other animals, but rather threats to our ego, self-esteem, and the grand story of who we are. In everyday life, it’s the psychological front on which we now feel continually endangered. It’s our “me” that has to constantly be on the lookout for danger.

All this to say, we have many good reasons to put ourselves at the center of the universe, and to see life through a personal lens of what it means for and about “me.”

The irony, however, is that our constant self-focus and the effort we put into shoring ourselves up, protecting and proving our self, promoting the “story” of our self, is actually an attempt to make ourselves feel happy and secure. And yet, it ends up making us feel unhappy and insecure. In the system we’ve designed, the relentless defense and hyper-vigilance to the self’s survival can never end. But in fact, it can… we just need to step out of the system.

In part 2, I’ll illuminate the subtle and not-so-subtle suffering that our “me” lens creates. I will also propose a radical solution. Spoiler alert: it will go against everything you’ve ever learned.

Share this post Facebook Bluesky Linkedin Email

Nancy Colier, LCSW, Rev., is a psychotherapist, interfaith minister, and the author of The Emotionally Exhausted Woman, Can’t Stop Thinking, The Power of Off, and Inviting a Monkey to Tea.

Alaska Airlines Launches 5 New Long-Haul Boeing 787 Routes: See Full List Now

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Journalist – Aaron is an aviation and travel journalist with 14 years of experience in the travel and tourism industry. His background spans airport operations, aviation infrastructure, and travel consulting, having worked with global organizations across New Zealand, Europe, and North America. Combining industry expertise with firsthand travel experience across more than 52 countries and five continents, Aaron covers airlines, airports, aviation policy, and the wider travel sector. He is based in Canterbury, New Zealand. His work has been published in Simple Flying and featured by MSN, SAM CHUI Aviation & Travel, EIN News, Pakistan Defence, and Climate Depot.

Alaska Airlines shows no signs of slowing its rapid international expansion. The airline, which received the Boeing 787-9 Dreamliner through its acquisition of Hawaiian Airlines, has expanded its international network to long-haul destinations from Seattle.

The carrier has operated its first international route to Tokyo Narita since May 2025, and while it began with Hawaiian Airlines' Airbus A330, it has now transitioned to the 787, which carries the full Alaska livery and onboard experience.

Despite its name, Alaska is based in Seattle and operates an extensive network of domestic and international flights from the Washington State airport. It has additional hubs across the US West Coast; however, all long-haul services operate to and from Seattle. The airline began its first 787-9 service in April, debuting a flight from Seattle to Rome.

The first long-haul international flight with the easily recognizable Aurora Borealis livery connects Seattle to Rome, with the service operating daily on a seasonal basis. This service operated from April through October, and will now hibernate over the winter travel period. Rome flights will resume in March 2027, according to data from Cirium, an online aviation analytics company.

Rome was selected as the airline's first long-haul European gateway to tap strong seasonal leisure demand in this unserved European market. Rome was the airport's biggest European market without a nonstop Seattle service. This gave Alaska a first-mover advantage, as no other airline at the time offered nonstop flights.

Seattle-Tacoma International Airport (SEA)

Leonardo da Vinci International Airport (FCO)

Alaska commenced its second long-haul 787-9 flight in May of this year, when the airline added London Heathrow Airport (LHR) to its network. Alaska made this route possible through a leasing agreement from oneworld alliance partner American Airlines for coveted Heathrow slots.

The service operates daily and enables passengers to connect seamlessly into the airline's wider network from Seattle. Alaska competes head-to-head with three other airlines on the city pair: British Airways, Delta Air Lines, and Virgin Atlantic.

Seattle-Tacoma International Airport (SEA)

Seattle-Tacoma International Airport (SEA)

While Alaska began service to Tokyo in 2025, the airline switched from the Hawaiian A330 to the 787-9 in October. This now sees the airline operating its first Asian services with the Boeing aircraft.

Hong Kong has emerged as a strong candidate as Alaska Airlines looks to build a 15‑route long‑haul plan and shift its fleet.

For summer 2027, Alaska will add two new international routes with the 787 widebody. These two seasonal services will again add more choice for travelers from the US West Coast. The Greek capital of Athens will be added to the airline's network from May 12, with initial plans to operate the seasonal service up to three times a week. No other airline operates the route nonstop.

Seattle-Tacoma International Airport (SEA)

Athens International Airport Eleftherios Venizelos (ATH)

Seattle-Tacoma International Airport (SEA)

Paris (CDG) will begin on May 25 and operate up to five times weekly through October. This nonstop link to the French capital will face direct competition from two SkyTeam carriers, as Air France serves Seattle year-round and Delta operates the route seasonally.

Where Alaska will decide to fly its 787 aircraft next is up for debate. The airline has been rumored to be considering additional destinations in Asia as part of its next step in developing Seattle into a world-class international hub. Australia could also be on the horizon, with many aviation enthusiasts hoping that Sydney could be the airline's first foray down under.

Pennsylvania 529 Investment Plan Review – Low-Cost Vanguard Portfolios, Best for Pennsylvanians

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Open the Pennsylvania 529 Investment Plan if you live in Pennsylvania, since your state tax deduction works with any 529 but only PA 529 savings don’t count against state financial aid. Elsewhere, compare it with cheaper plans Morningstar also rates Gold, and pick your own state’s plan if its tax break requires it.

The Pennsylvania 529 Investment Plan is Pennsylvania’s 529 plan open to savers in any state, and its 26 portfolios, including target-enrollment ones that shift toward bonds as college nears, are all managed by Vanguard. At an assumed 6% yearly return, saving $200 a month for 18 years in a target-enrollment portfolio costs about $1,000 in fees, against about $1,700 at the 0.30% that plans sold without an advisor averaged in 2025, Morningstar found.

Pennsylvanians should open it, since their deduction works with any 529 but only PA 529 savings stay out of state aid calculations. Savers elsewhere should start with their own state’s plan when its tax break requires it, and otherwise can pay about half as much in fees at Bright Start.

Clarke: 'I am world class, I was just waiting for the world to see'

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

"Chris Billam-Smith is a tough guy, and in boxing it's not the place to be tough." Cheavon Clarke reflects on his stunning victory over the former world champion; Watch Aaron McKenna vs Jevgenijs Aleksejevs on Friday December 4 live on Sky Sports

Sports Journalist @johndennen

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Cheavon Clarke catapulted himself into the top tier of the cruiserweight division with his stunning stoppage victory over Chris Billam-Smith.

Billam-Smith is a highly-experienced world champion, well known for hard-fought victories over the likes of Lawrence Okolie and Richard Riakporhe.

"I've always been world class. It's just waiting on the world to catch up and see it. That fight gave me the opportunity to do that. We're getting there," Clarke told Sky Sports.

When they fought at the Copper Box Arena earlier this month, Clarke did something no one had managed before - he dropped Billam-Smith (twice in fact) and stopped him.

"To Chris' detriment, he's a tough guy and in boxing it's not the place to be tough," Clarke said. "History is a great tell of the future. Every tough guy has a day where he's not tough no more. Unfortunately that was Chris' day."

Clarke has come back from defeat himself and doesn't rule out Billam-Smith doing the same.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"It doesn't mean that that's the end of it for Chris. Because he's been a world champion, he's got the experience, he can carry on," Clarke said.

"People need to stop writing him off. I've seen some comments online, just wild. It's not my business to be defending him, I'm not his lawyer… You just need to be factual."

Though he does expect Billam-Smith to take plenty of time to recover rather than pursue a rematch.

"There was talks of this after the fight, but who knows? What does it do for Chev's career right now, bearing in mind that I'm currently ranked above him," Clarke said.

"I'm looking to fight the guys above me and trying to make what makes sense for me."

Watch Aaron McKenna vs Jevgenijs Aleksejevs on Friday December 4 live on Sky Sports.

Stefan Borson: Aston Villa 'well placed' to benefit from NBA expansion

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Aston Villa could benefit from a new revenue stream due to the owners' connections with the National Basketball Association (NBA).

That is according to former Manchester City financial adviser Stefan Borson, who exclusively told Football Insider that discussions over the NBA's European adventure are likely already advanced.

Sky Sports reported on Monday, 16 November, that the NBA is looking to expand into Europe, backed by billions of dollars worth of funding.

There is a lot of excitement around a bigger basketball culture coming to Europe, and the Villans could certainly benefit from it.

Villa co-owner Wes Edens has plenty of experience in American sports, having owned the Milwaukee Bucks since 2014.

Many at Villa Park have expected Edens to recreate the same success in the Premier League, and the Europa League title has been a close second.

Speaking exclusively to Football Insider, Borson believes that the links between Edens and the NBA could almost certainly benefit Villa as talks over a European expansion begin.

"There's already a lot of relationships around Europe with the local football clubs, like Fenerbahce or Barcelona. They all have their own basketball teams," he said.

"I would think there's a really good chance that if the European NBA is serious and wants a number of teams in England, Villa are going to be very well placed to join.

"To be honest, we're probably quite a long way down the line of discussions, and I suspect a lot of certainly exploratory conversations are happening with the NBA if they press ahead with this European NBA."

Hosting games for the European NBA would be more than exciting, that is if Villa can build a venue to hold such sporting events.

Villa's owners continue to play a key role behind the scenes in B6, while several other Premier League clubs are wanting a change at the highest level.

Their latest move has been to tie Unai Emery down to new terms at Villa Park, as the talented manager continues to be linked with deals elsewhere.

Despite the inability to splash so much cash, Emery never panics and always brings Villa back to where they should be in competitions.

The owners are only going to continue making these smart moves behind the scenes, and that could be the case with this European NBA venture.

Trump initially accepted the midterm results, but most GOP voters doubt they were fair

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A firefighter walks past voting booths at a firehouse serving as a polling location in Detroit on Nov. 3. Spencer Platt/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump may have initially accepted the results of his party's midterm losses, but data released Friday shows there is still broad distrust in the elections process among Republicans.

Less than half of Republican voters nationwide considered the midterm elections "free and fair," according to new polling by the University of California San Diego. Just 52% of Republicans said they trusted votes were counted accurately.

In comparison, after the 2024 election that Trump won, more than 80% of Republicans said they had confidence in the vote-counting process.

While trust in elections often dips for voters of the losing party, the GOP has seen bigger swings in confidence in recent years, as Trump has falsely claimed that U.S. elections are corrupt.

"There are still a lot of people who remain unconvinced that they can trust elections," said Thad Kousser, a co-director of UC San Diego's Center for Transparent and Trusted Elections. "And they're predominantly located in one [political] party."

Overall, a wide majority — 66% — of Americans said they think the midterms were fair. That represents an improvement from before the election, when less than half of U.S. adults said they expected free and fair contests.

But that post-election increase in trust was buoyed by Democrats and independents — many of whom worried before Election Day about potential federal interference at the polls that didn't come to pass.

"It would be a mistake to [look at the 66%] and think, 'Oh, everyone has a pretty good view of the election,'" said Lauren Prather, also a co-director of UC San Diego's election center. "It really buries the lede a little bit about the partisan gap."

The widespread doubt among Republicans may be a reality check for those wondering whether Trump's brand of election denial has run its course in American politics.

On the one hand, there will be fewer politicians who deny or question election results in office next year. And the vast majority of election-denying candidates running for positions involved in overseeing statewide elections lost their races this year, according to tracking by the advocacy group States United Action.

Election experts have also celebrated as Trump and high-profile losing candidates on the ballot this year conceded their losses.

"Sure, I'll accept [the results]," Trump told Fox News' Bret Baier on election night, as it became clear Democrats would retake the House and Senate. "Why wouldn't I?"

But just a few days later, he began conspiracy theorizing about those same results.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest," Trump posted online.

The new polling from UC San Diego shows that among Republicans, there still may be a receptive audience to such claims. When asked if politicians are discussing voter fraud too much, too little or about the right amount, just 15% of Republicans answered "too much."

"There is both demand among the Republican base for continued focus on this issue, and as we saw even after the election, politicians are continuing to supply it," Kousser said. "As long as those two dynamics are there, there's still going to be a market for these concerns."

To be clear, in most elections — domestically and internationally — supporters of the losing party usually see a dip in trust.

"It's sort of like asking people after an overtime basketball game, do you trust the referees?" Kousser said.

But in an ideal world, he said that oscillation would be more like 10 percentage points in either direction. Not the 30-point swing seen among Republicans between 2024 and 2026.

The numbers do still represent an improvement from 2020, however. At that time, leading up to the Jan. 6 riot at the U.S. Capitol in 2021, just 22% of Republicans said they had confidence in the nationwide vote count, according to similar polling done by MIT's Election Data and Science Lab.

American Airlines Says Operations Chief David Seymour Will Retire — With Dismissal-Style Severance

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

American Airlines chief operating officer David Seymour is leaving the job December 31. CEO Robert Isom calls it a retirement. The agreement American signed with Seymour gives him severance on terms normally reserved for dismissal without cause or resignation for good reason. American’s last proxy put the cash severance alone at $2,750,625 for a hypothetical departure on December 31, 2025, with total value over $7 million. The airline hasn’t disclosed the final amount for this exit. [T]he Company will provide Mr. Seymour with the severance payments and benefits pursuant to the covered termination provisions set forth in his previously disclosed Severance Agreement with the Company dated as of September 20, 2023

[T]he Company will provide Mr. Seymour with the severance payments and benefits pursuant to the covered termination provisions set forth in his previously disclosed Severance Agreement with the Company dated as of September 20, 2023

JonCarlo “JC” Gulbranson takes over January 1, reporting directly to Isom. He’s the executive I flagged as Seymour’s likely successor in August, when American handed him much of the job.

Aviation watchdog JonNYC shared Isom’s message to employees. Isom thanks Seymour for his friendship and his work bringing US Airways and American together. Before joining America West in 1999, Seymour led a platoon in the 82nd Airborne. Just last month he went back to West Point and Fort Bragg with Medal of Honor recipients to meet the cadets and soldiers who followed him there.

AA pic.twitter.com/9919XVOKrW — JonNYC (@xJonNYC) November 20, 2026

Under Seymour’s contract, cash severance is 18 months of salary plus a year and a half of target bonus. He also gets money for 18 months of health premiums, and his stock can keep vesting during those 18 months. Between cash, stock, health premiums and flight benefits he’d already earned, American’s illustration in its latest proxy came to $7.13 million. Separately, Seymour remains eligible for his 2026 annual bonus. His advisory job pays $25,000 a month for up to three months ($75,000), plus executive benefits. His contract calculates severance using pay immediately before termination. Seymour has to sign a release and honor his existing restrictions, and American says it’ll file the full transition letter with its annual report. Those flight benefits include lifetime confirmed personal travel in any cabin for Seymour and his immediate family, Admirals Club access and another 12 confirmed roundtrips a year for friends and other relatives. The Handoff Started In August When American put Heather Garboden in charge of the customer experience, they gave her two bosses. She was supposed to persuade customers to pay more to fly American while reporting both to commercial and to the executive responsible for getting flights off the gate on time. When a pilot asked Seymour in 2022 what American was doing about poor customer satisfaction, his answer was “I think we’re on a good path to get there.” He went on to talk about running the operation. What was Garboden supposed to do when the catering truck was late and the choice was a delayed departure or six hours in first class without meals? In August Garboden began reporting solely to chief commercial officer Nat Pieper and picked up catering, reservations and service recovery. Pieper also got marketing, brand, advertising and partnerships as American broke up Global Engagement head Ron DeFeo’s group. Caroline Clayton took corporate communications. Then I reported network chief Brian Znotins was leaving as Pieper set out to rebuild flying in big cities where American had pulled back. Seymour actually picked up American Eagle in August. But Gulbranson got the airports and operations planning on top of flight operations, flight attendants and the control center. Now he gets safety, regulatory compliance and aircraft maintenance, too. Corporate security goes to government affairs chief Steve Neuman. American will tell employees in December who gets the regionals. If this hadn’t actually been decided already, then August was Gulbranson’s audition. Isom’s memo introduces the retirement as the next step in the August reorganization. Seymour’s contract even lists a material reduction in responsibilities as grounds that can qualify an executive for severance. We don’t know who first proposed retirement, but this looks to me like a negotiated handoff that was already well underway. American Had To Retrofit Planes It Had Just Retrofitted While there are numerous stories like it – such as insufficient oversight of outsourced Maintenance, Repair, and Overhaul – I always think of a few things about Seymour’s tenure. One is explaining why the airline wouldn’t invest in RFID baggage tags like Delta despite perpetually bottom of the industry performance in mishandled bags. Another is failing to fix ‘Concept D’ business class seats that rocked. And the biggest is mismanaging cabin retrofits of the domestic fleet. Seymour was running integrated operations when the first American Airlines 737 MAX introduced the cabin that would go into its existing 737s and Airbus A321s. A flight attendant eventually asked whether anyone had built a mockup before committing to it. They hadn’t. Doug Parker said network planning had largely driven the design. Seymour called it “a mistake that should not have happened” and later explained what American had changed. For subsequent cabins they’d bring in real seats, build overhead bins and try pushing a service cart through. In his words, “We do mockups and not just you know ‘tape it out.’” Flight attendants working in the back galley had to help passengers get out of the lavatories because the two doors opened into each other and caught. The sinks were too shallow for the water pressure. I washed my hands on the inaugural flight and got water all over my shirt and pants, as did everyone else. Up front, the seat supports took up the space where a first class passenger was supposed to put a bag. American was still installing the interior after deciding to fix it. Project Oasis put the new seats into existing 737s. Then Project Kodiak went back into planes they’d already redone to fix the storage, give the first row some legroom back and add the device holders coach passengers already had. How Well Did American Actually Run? After the troubled summer of 2019, American gave Seymour responsibility for all operations. The following June he got the chief operating officer title and added flight service. For years my frustration was that when the operation fell short, the answer seemed to be to give the executive running it a bigger job. In 2023 American completed its highest share of scheduled flights since the merger and edged out United on domestic on-time arrivals. And spreading the Dallas schedule from nine daily rushes to 13 smaller ones helped passengers this year. The hub chief reported half as many missed connections in the first 17 days. But by 2025 American ranked eighth of ten network airlines for on-time arrivals. And the Transportation Department’s new figures show that Delta and United both beat American every single month from January through September 2026. Counting domestic flights including regional partners, American came in at 73.30% on time, United at 76.20% and Delta at 78.31%. American canceled 46,059 flights, or 2.99% of its schedule, against 1.75% at United and 2.25% at Delta. Credit: American Airlines One clue to what management valued is in that same proxy. In August 2025 the board gave Seymour a retention stock award worth about $2 million at target. The entire award turns on avoiding cancellations American counts as controllable. A flight that runs three hours late still counts as a success on that measure. The award measures performance through June 2027, so it can still pay out after he’s gone. Gulbranson is well regarded inside American. He started at Northwest, joined US Airways in 2008 and helped integrate flight operations systems during the merger. He’s run both the control center that decides what to cancel and the customer care operation that hears from the people who get stranded. Credit: American Airlines Listen to the two men describe January’s Winter Storm Fern. Seymour said the technology worked as intended and that he hadn’t seen reports of flight attendants sleeping in airports even as his boss Isom acknowledged crew had slept on floors. Gulbranson went before the pilots’ union board and told them where management had misjudged the storm and overestimated how quickly they could recover. The union’s account called his assessment “unvarnished.” That’s one reason I’m glad he’ll be running the operation the next time it snows in Dallas and up the East Coast. American will tell employees in December who gets the regionals.

If this hadn’t actually been decided already, then August was Gulbranson’s audition. Isom’s memo introduces the retirement as the next step in the August reorganization. Seymour’s contract even lists a material reduction in responsibilities as grounds that can qualify an executive for severance. We don’t know who first proposed retirement, but this looks to me like a negotiated handoff that was already well underway.

While there are numerous stories like it – such as insufficient oversight of outsourced Maintenance, Repair, and Overhaul – I always think of a few things about Seymour’s tenure. One is explaining why the airline wouldn’t invest in RFID baggage tags like Delta despite perpetually bottom of the industry performance in mishandled bags. Another is failing to fix ‘Concept D’ business class seats that rocked. And the biggest is mismanaging cabin retrofits of the domestic fleet. Seymour was running integrated operations when the first American Airlines 737 MAX introduced the cabin that would go into its existing 737s and Airbus A321s. A flight attendant eventually asked whether anyone had built a mockup before committing to it. They hadn’t. Doug Parker said network planning had largely driven the design. Seymour called it “a mistake that should not have happened” and later explained what American had changed. For subsequent cabins they’d bring in real seats, build overhead bins and try pushing a service cart through. In his words, “We do mockups and not just you know ‘tape it out.’” Flight attendants working in the back galley had to help passengers get out of the lavatories because the two doors opened into each other and caught. The sinks were too shallow for the water pressure. I washed my hands on the inaugural flight and got water all over my shirt and pants, as did everyone else. Up front, the seat supports took up the space where a first class passenger was supposed to put a bag. American was still installing the interior after deciding to fix it. Project Oasis put the new seats into existing 737s. Then Project Kodiak went back into planes they’d already redone to fix the storage, give the first row some legroom back and add the device holders coach passengers already had. How Well Did American Actually Run? After the troubled summer of 2019, American gave Seymour responsibility for all operations. The following June he got the chief operating officer title and added flight service. For years my frustration was that when the operation fell short, the answer seemed to be to give the executive running it a bigger job. In 2023 American completed its highest share of scheduled flights since the merger and edged out United on domestic on-time arrivals. And spreading the Dallas schedule from nine daily rushes to 13 smaller ones helped passengers this year. The hub chief reported half as many missed connections in the first 17 days. But by 2025 American ranked eighth of ten network airlines for on-time arrivals. And the Transportation Department’s new figures show that Delta and United both beat American every single month from January through September 2026. Counting domestic flights including regional partners, American came in at 73.30% on time, United at 76.20% and Delta at 78.31%. American canceled 46,059 flights, or 2.99% of its schedule, against 1.75% at United and 2.25% at Delta. Credit: American Airlines One clue to what management valued is in that same proxy. In August 2025 the board gave Seymour a retention stock award worth about $2 million at target. The entire award turns on avoiding cancellations American counts as controllable. A flight that runs three hours late still counts as a success on that measure. The award measures performance through June 2027, so it can still pay out after he’s gone. Gulbranson is well regarded inside American. He started at Northwest, joined US Airways in 2008 and helped integrate flight operations systems during the merger. He’s run both the control center that decides what to cancel and the customer care operation that hears from the people who get stranded. Credit: American Airlines Listen to the two men describe January’s Winter Storm Fern. Seymour said the technology worked as intended and that he hadn’t seen reports of flight attendants sleeping in airports even as his boss Isom acknowledged crew had slept on floors. Gulbranson went before the pilots’ union board and told them where management had misjudged the storm and overestimated how quickly they could recover. The union’s account called his assessment “unvarnished.” That’s one reason I’m glad he’ll be running the operation the next time it snows in Dallas and up the East Coast. After the troubled summer of 2019, American gave Seymour responsibility for all operations. The following June he got the chief operating officer title and added flight service. For years my frustration was that when the operation fell short, the answer seemed to be to give the executive running it a bigger job.

In 2023 American completed its highest share of scheduled flights since the merger and edged out United on domestic on-time arrivals. And spreading the Dallas schedule from nine daily rushes to 13 smaller ones helped passengers this year. The hub chief reported half as many missed connections in the first 17 days.

But by 2025 American ranked eighth of ten network airlines for on-time arrivals. And the Transportation Department’s new figures show that Delta and United both beat American every single month from January through September 2026. Counting domestic flights including regional partners, American came in at 73.30% on time, United at 76.20% and Delta at 78.31%. American canceled 46,059 flights, or 2.99% of its schedule, against 1.75% at United and 2.25% at Delta.

One clue to what management valued is in that same proxy. In August 2025 the board gave Seymour a retention stock award worth about $2 million at target. The entire award turns on avoiding cancellations American counts as controllable. A flight that runs three hours late still counts as a success on that measure. The award measures performance through June 2027, so it can still pay out after he’s gone.

Gulbranson is well regarded inside American. He started at Northwest, joined US Airways in 2008 and helped integrate flight operations systems during the merger. He’s run both the control center that decides what to cancel and the customer care operation that hears from the people who get stranded.

Listen to the two men describe January’s Winter Storm Fern. Seymour said the technology worked as intended and that he hadn’t seen reports of flight attendants sleeping in airports even as his boss Isom acknowledged crew had slept on floors.

Gulbranson went before the pilots’ union board and told them where management had misjudged the storm and overestimated how quickly they could recover. The union’s account called his assessment “unvarnished.” That’s one reason I’m glad he’ll be running the operation the next time it snows in Dallas and up the East Coast.

Until Isom and his gang of America West misfits are gone this is nothing more than rearranging the deck chairs on the Titanic.

AA is finally addressing the chronic issues that have hurt AA and passenger reason for avoiding them.

DFW is a sprawling mess that AA cannot yet manage anywhere near as effectively as DL manages ATL while CLT is overscheduled.

The most recent DOT data, however, shows that AA is operating far closer to UA than it has in the past. AA’s on-time is within a couple percent of UA – both of which are multiple points behind DL which leads the industry in on-time according to the most recent DOT data.

It is UA that has the industry’s worst baggage even though AA is right above them.

The real improvement is in AA’s invol DB performance which is now below industry average. in fact, it is F9 that singularly is below industry average showing how rare invol DBs are on other carriers – and non-existent on DL even though DL has almost 2X more voluntary DBs than AA.

also worth noting that, for the 3rd quarter, DL led the industry in domestic enplanements, taking the lead from AA a year ago. I suspect it is precisely AA’s poor handling of IROPS that cost it the title of being the US’ largest domestic airline.

and for those that still cling to old advertising, WN was the 4th largest US airline based on domestic enplanements in the 3rd quarter.

Türkiye Just Announced a New Golden Visa Starting at $150,000

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Türkiye is one of the world’s most visited countries, straddling the vibrant crossroads of Europe, Asia, and the Middle East. With sun-soaked Mediterranean beaches, Ottoman-influenced gastronomy, historic mosques, and bustling bazaars, it’s no surprise that the country attracted nearly 64 million visitors in 2025. But if your love of the country has you considering a prolonged vacation (or perhaps a second home), the government’s new golden visa might be a good fit—so long as you have $150,000 to spend.

On November 17, Türkiye's president Recep Tayyip Erdoğan signed a decision to enact a new foreign investor visa. The program offers two pathways: a $150,000 investment for a three-year residence permit, or $200,000 for a five-year stay (both of which can extend to the applicant’s spouse and dependents).

As for where your money goes, you can deposit your funds in a local bank, buy government debt instruments, contribute to a pension plan, or invest in real estate or venture capital funds. No matter which pathway you select, you must hold your investment for at least half the visa term (as in, 1.5 years for the three-year visa or 2.5 years for the five-year visa). That means you could potentially withdraw your investment as early as after 18 months, according to IMI Daily, an investment migration trade publication.

The president’s announcement doesn't detail when or how foreigners can begin to apply, or the specific documents required. However, applications for the country’s other residence-by-investment permits require documents such as proof of financial means and health insurance, according to the government’s Investment and Finance Office.

If you don’t want to shell out $150,000, Türkiye offers other (renewable) relocation pathways. You may qualify for a two-year Short-Term Residence Permit for reasons such as tourism and university-sponsored research. Or, if your spouse is Turkish, you could apply for the three-year Family Residence Permit. Once you’ve lived in the country continuously for eight years, you’ll be eligible to apply for the indefinite Long-Term Residence Permit, which grants you almost all the same perks as citizenship, minus civic duties such as the ability to vote and mandatory military service.

It's important to note that the new golden visa does not provide permanent citizenship. If you’re hoping to find your way into a passport, get ready to spend more. Türkiye’s citizenship-by-investment pipeline will require you to buy a home worth at least $400,000, invest at least $500,000 in sectors such as government bonds or venture capital funds, or, alternatively, help create at least 50 jobs.

Over the last few years, the influx of new residency-by-investment programs has made it easier for wealthy travelers to relocate internationally. In New Zealand, you can invest $1 million New Zealand Dollars (about $567,300) in exchange for a three-year work-to-residency track. For Mediterranean living, Greece offers a five-year permit for a 250,000 euro (about $291,120) investment in a startup. Or, for a more affordable option, Paraguay has a permanent residency program for those who can invest $150,000 in “tourism projects” or $200,000 in stocks or real estate—putting it in line with Türkiye as one of the more affordable relocation options.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Man Utd fan criticism has gone too far at times - Berrada

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Manchester United chief executive Omar Berrada (left) with director of football Jason Wilcox

Manchester United chief executive Omar Berrada says some of the criticism aimed at the club's hierarchy has "crossed a line".

In a rare interview to Sky Sports as head coach Mauricio Pochettino prepares for his first game in charge, at Liverpool on Sunday, Berrada said he "understood" the frustration of supporters at United being 18th in the Premier League. He also said some of the criticism was "fair".

However, he feels some of it has gone too far.

"Being in this position comes naturally with the criticism and scrutiny we're going to get," he said. "We accept it. Some of it is fair, some of it does cross the line.

"We do live in this world of social media, where people like to generate negative stories about the club because it drives more engagement, and it drives more general noise and activity around the club. But we park that."

United's director of football Jason Wilcox has been singled out by supporters at recent games. There have also been social media calls for both Wilcox and Berrada to lose their jobs.

In addition, songs are now sung regularly calling for minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe to leave, while opposition to the Glazer family has remained intense since their leveraged buyout in 2005 saddled debt on to the club that now exceeds Â£1bn.

Andrey Santos has started four Premier League games for Manchester United since his Â£50m summer move from Chelsea

Wilcox, in particular, has been blamed for what is perceived to have been poor summer recruitment. After the 2-0 defeat by Atletico Madrid in September, defender Lisandro Martinez said he "expected more from the technical director".

Ousted boss Michael Carrick felt he was let down by a transfer strategy that targeted midfield, while not addressing issues at left-back and in attack - which has been compounded by injuries to Luke Shaw and Benjamin Sesko.

Carrick wanted to target Elliot Anderson and Mateus Fernandes, but United backed off when Nottingham Forest and West Ham's demands went higher than they wanted to pay. Anderson eventually joined Manchester City for Â£116m, with Fernandes moving to Tottenham for Â£85m.

Instead, United paid a combined Â£118m for Carlos Baleba and Andrey Santos, who have started seven Premier League games between then, and triggered Youri Tielemans' Â£35m release clause at Aston Villa. Tielemans scored his first United goal in the 4-4 draw with Aston Villa on 8 November and has one assist.

However, Berrada has defended United's transfer business, saying: "When we look at the summer transfer window of 2026, I know we're receiving a lot of criticism for it, but we're actually happy with the players we bought.

"We think that Carlos Baleba, Youri Tielemans, Andrey Santos, and Karl Darlow - and let's not forget that we've also welcomed back Marcus Rashford, who had a very good season on loan at Barcelona - these are five additions that we think have made the squad fundamentally better.

"We went in knowing what we needed to do. Of course, there have been different dynamics at play and we've said consistently in the last few years we need to stay disciplined. We need to take the right decisions. We need to buy the right players, for the right value at the right time - in terms of what the squad needs.

"When we find the right opportunities, we will spend. But what we don't want to be doing is spending just for the sake of it. So, could we have signed an additional defender to straighten the depth and the quality? Yes, but we have to find the right players at the right value.

"There's no point in us spending Â£40m, Â£50m for players we're not convinced are going to add value to the team."

When he spoke to the wider media for the first time, 30 minutes before Berrada's interview was aired, Pochettino said he hoped to have a say in all areas of the playing side, even though his official title is head coach.

"I know that, being a head coach, I am the guy that is going to decide the squad for Sunday," he said. "I'm going to pick the starting 11 and I'm going to make the changes.

"Of course, in the future, I would like to be involved in all the main decisions that will happen on the squad."

Berrada said there had been little option other than to dismiss Carrick given United's plight and the team had "dropped off in many areas".

Pochettino has spoken individually to virtually every player to get an understanding of what the issues are.

He said he was wary of reading too much into data that shows United's players have not been running enough, arguing "if you don't put context on the data, it's very dangerous to talk".

The 54-year-old also said that, despite talking extensively about culture in his first in-house interview on Thursday, it could take 10 years to build one.

"The culture of the club and the identity of the club is very clear," he said. "It's a historic club, one of the best in the world. It's difficult to find another club the size of Manchester United.

"The culture of the club and the identity of the club is very clear. It needs to be in a different place than we are now."

Pochettino did confirm Bruno Fernandes will remain as skipper for the trip to Liverpool.

Fernandes has struggled for form and, with the 32-year-old's contract due to expire in the summer, there has been no breakthrough in negotiations over an extension.

United do have the option to trigger an additional year on the Portugal playmaker's present deal but, given the massive dip from the levels that earned him all last season's major player of the year awards, there are some who wonder whether a clean break might be best.

Pochettino preferred to deflect the wider aspect of Fernandes' future and concentrate on the immediate.

"Bruno is our captain and he's a very important player," he said.

"I think we will have time after to talk about the [other] things that you are asking me."

Senate passes first-ever prediction market bill as Democrats turn up heat on upstart industry

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Prediction markets are facing new limits on Capitol Hill. The Senate passed a bill Thursday evening that would bar platforms from offering contracts tied to terrorism, assassination, an individual’s death, war or U.S. military operations–the first legislation targeting the burgeoning industry to clear either chamber of Congress.

Sens. Adam Schiff (D-Calif.) and John Curtis (R-Utah) introduced the bill, formally called the DEATH BETS Act, to prevent exchanges and clearinghouses overseen by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission from offering or processing those contracts. It would also ban contracts whose outcomes are reasonably expected to depend mainly on someone’s death, even if the wording does not mention death.

The bill passed the Senate by a voice vote and must still pass the House before heading to the president’s desk.

“They are offered with no regard for national security and in far too many cases, even basic morality,” Schiff said on the Senate floor. “These markets are operating in a wild West, desperately in need of regulation and guardrails.”

As platforms like Kalshi and Polymarket have grown in prominence over the past two years, they have offered contracts on seemingly anything, drawing scrutiny over bets tied to war, violence or human suffering. They have also raised concerns about insider trading, as users can trade without publicly revealing their identities, making it harder for outsiders to spot potential conflicts of interest.

Several cases have drawn politicians’ attention to these issues. One involved a Polymarket trader who made more than $400,000 betting on Nicolás Maduro’s removal from power before U.S. forces captured the Venezuelan leader in January. Kalshi also drew scrutiny over a market on whether Iranian Supreme Leader, Ali Khamenei, would leave office, which sparked a dispute after his killing when Kalshi declined to pay traders who had bet he would leave office, citing a rule that excluded death as a qualifying outcome.

In his speech, Schiff pointed to a Polymarket bet on whether NASA’s Artemis II mission would explode, raising concerns about wagers on astronauts’ lives. He also cited a bet on whether Russian forces would capture the Ukrainian city of Myrnohrad.

The companies’ leaders have previously pushed back on these criticisms. “We don’t list markets directly tied to death,” Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour wrote on X in February. Speaking at a conference in March, Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan called conflict-related markets a “complicated question” but defended their value as a source of useful information.

Kalshi and Polymarket did not immediately respond to Fortune’s request for comment.

Other platforms, such as Novig, avoid these contracts altogether, limiting their offerings to sports markets and restricting trading to users 21 and older.

The bill’s passage comes amid growing political scrutiny of prediction markets, particularly after Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress in this month’s midterm elections. Over the past week, several Democratic lawmakers have criticized the platforms on social media.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) pointed Wednesday to Kalshi’s decision to pause trading on contracts tied to President Donald Trump’s public remarks, which have drawn concerns about insider trading. “The CFTC needs to do more to stop insiders from cashing in,” she wrote on X.

Rep. Ro Khanna (D-Calif.) focused on younger users, calling Wednesday for an investigation and a ban on sports prediction markets. He criticized Kalshi for allowing 18-year-olds to bet on sports and said users under 21 had wagered $3.9 billion on the platform. “They call it a ‘prediction market.’ It’s gambling,” he wrote.

Though Democrats have been the most vocal, the scrutiny has crossed party lines. Besides cosponsoring the bill that passed Thursday, Curtis joined Warren last month in questioning Kalshi and Polymarket about potentially improper election-related trading. The Utah Republican has also backed separate legislation to ban sports and casino-style prediction contracts, citing concerns about young users’ exposure to gambling.

Sen. Ted Cruz (R-Texas) also announced plans to introduce legislation that would bar prediction market platforms from offering sports contracts without state or tribal approval, according to Bloomberg Law.

We Really Need to Talk About the <i><em>MobLand</em></i> Season 2 Finale

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A certain character’s fate hangs in the balance.

Nothing is simple in MobLand. Two seasons in, the plot has racked up more lines than Eddie put up his nose the night he stabbed Tommy Stevenson in season 1. Actually, that’s not strictly true. One thing is simple in MobLand: Eddie. He is a simpleton of truly Homeric (as in Simpson) proportions: a smarmy, sneering, simpering Freudian mess, channelled with gimlet-eyed precision by Anson Boon. All he wants is his God-given right to be respected.

Trouble is, when you’re an anti-Midas who turns everything he touches to shit, respect is a dish that never leaves the kitchen. Eddie is, in Kevin’s words in season 1, “a knifey little twerp.” That stabbing set the whole bloody saga in motion, so it’s only right that two seasons later we end with Eddie the Incompetent. Full circle, then, like a roundabout made of corpses.

MobLand opened with Eddie turning a stabbing into a gang war, so it seems fitting that we end with Eddie turning an assassination into an own goal. But we’ll get to that.

It seems Conrad’s chickens are coming home to roost, and his marbles are jangling around his head like loose change. We open the episode with his card being declined at a petrol station. In frustration, he shoots the till boy dead. But he knows what it means.

“We’re getting lifted,” he tells Maeve in the car. Police are raiding the entire business: bank accounts are frozen, warrants are out, and Tattersall is telling reporters the Harrigans have links to terror groups across the globe. The family convenes at a dingy old safe house out of town.

“We’re terrorists,” says Seraphina. “We’re looking at life in Guantánamo. It’s now or never.”

Her plan has been bubbling for a while. She calls it Harrigan 2.0: going global.

Remember the diplomatic suitcase of diamonds Seraphina collected from her billionaire stepdad Charlton in the last episode? It was meant to fund her and Bella’s takeover of the Harrigan operation. Now, it seems, Harry has it. He takes her aside to let her know he knows. “How did you know?” she asks, incredulous.

Then he says the three words he says more than any others in MobLand: “It’s my job.”

Inside the safe house, the family are all there, and everyone has a plan to pitch. Kevin wants to crawl back to Kat and hand over Conrad, Maeve, and Eddie. “Clean the house,” he growls to Harry, out of earshot of the others.

Conrad, meanwhile, isn’t sure what to do. “What are our options?” he asks Harry.

“We don’t have any,” comes the terse reply. “Either we run seven ways and it’s every man and woman for themselves, or we go back to Kat and try to strike some kind of deal.”

Kevin proposes offering her 50 percent and first working out who’s a liability. “And right now I’m seeing a lot of liabilities. Right, Harry?”

Bella has had it up to ’ere with Kevin’s pusillanimity. “Oh, you nearly got to the end of a sentence without saying ‘Harry,’” she taunts. “I know he saved you from a bumming once but… Jesus.”

Kevin pulls a pistol and fires, missing her ear by millimeters. Conrad draws. “Put the gun down, boy,” he hisses. Bella steps forward. “Go on then, big boy.”

You could cut the tension with piano wire.

“Fuck this, I’m getting us out of this mess,” says Seraphina, defusing the standoff by fetching a fistful of diamonds from the car. “Fifteen Type IIa brilliants. North of ten million euros,” she tells Conrad, placing them in his hand.

The plan: offer Kat a cut of the empire. “This time tomorrow we’re back in business.”

Remember that glorious, long-winded speech Conrad gave in Spain about the good old days, how he came from nothing, and how Maeve has been his compass through the carnage of his life? “We were born outlaws and we’ll die outlaws,” he bellowed.

Well, now he knows the jig is up. Looking balefully at the floor, he grunts: “Do it.”

So how will Harry play this? He slips out to his 4x4 and rings Kat. “At 5 p.m. I will meet your diplomatic courier. I will pass to said courier €10 million in untraceable diamonds. Upon authentication, I will give you the location of the Harrigan family.”

A double cross? It must be a ruse. He simply MUST have a plan… Then, hanging up, he mutters: “Fuck this, I’m done.”

He returns to the family and lies: “Kat will take the ten million and 50 percent.” Too easy by half.

As Frankie put it two episodes ago: “Is that it? Unless you’ve got some brilliant plans, that is actually a bit shit. What are you up to, Harry?”

We’re more than halfway through and haven’t heard a peep from Frankie, our laconic, crocodilian villain. Ah, but here he is, getting the news of Harry’s volte-face from Tattersall. “How do we know it’s not a bluff?”

“He’s not stupid,” says Tattersall. “He knows when the game’s up.”

“Well, you know me,” leers Frankie. “I can’t resist a gamble.” Guns are grabbed from car boots, and off he pootles with a gaggle of tooled-up goons to send the Harrigans back to Hell.

It’s all unfolding very quickly now, as denouements always should. Back at the safe house, Conrad is still seeing the dead, including the petrol station lad he just murdered. Maeve says he’s been saying their names in his sleep.

The old loon’s marbles are now departing his head like passengers from a cancelled flight. But there’s one thing that’ll straighten any man out: a black pearl of Maeve wisdom. “We’ve been stuck up tighter holes than Kat McAllister’s,” she reassures him. “But when you’re stuck up someone’s hole, there’s at least two ways out.”

Quite so. And who better to lead the way than Harry? As ever, he picks the exit that leads straight back to where he came in. Unbeknownst to us, he has gone up Kat’s backside, rummaged about, and come out the other end holding her entire career. The “betrayal” was a ruse from the start.

So when Frankie’s goons arrive armed to the teeth, it’s a trap: flash grenades, smoke grenades, poison gas, and Zosia on the exit with a machine gun for anyone daft enough to run. “Oh, Harry,” concedes Frankie, before a mighty gunfight erupts and Harry tears through the house like Keyser Söze in a gas mask. “So this isn’t the beginning of a beautiful friendship,” smiles Frankie, choking in the corner. Harry shoots him in the knee.

Then Harry rings Kat. Those “untraceable” diamonds? Very traceable indeed, straight to a terrorist organization. He has bribed a low-ranking diplomatic courier to deliver them along with a “little present, just for you.”

She opens the box, ashen-faced. It’s Donnie’s blue and rotting head. “This one you might find harder to justify,” he tells her.

“My family are gonna hunt you down and feed you your balls,” she spits.

“Probably,” says Harry. “But not before they feed you yours.”

Checkmate, Kat. The black queen has no more squares to go.

But wait … Harry can offer her one square. “I can fix this,” he says.

Here’s what she’s going to do. She’ll resign. She’ll kill the police investigation. The Harrigans’ accounts will reopen and they’ll come off the terror list.

Cut to a London corridor of power, where a faceless civil servant informs DS Kavanagh that “continuation of your terrorism financing line will interfere with an active joint intelligence operation between British and American security services. You will stand down at the close of business.”

And so, full circle. Maeve is electrocuting Frankie’s balls with a car battery. Kevin is building bridges with Bella. Conrad is going madder by the day. And Eddie?

The Semtex he blackmailed Paul for was meant for Harry, at Maeve’s behest. In the hands of any other Harrigan, this would be a doddle. But not Eddie. Not the knifey little twerp, a man who could trip over his own foreskin in an empty car park.

And he does. But first, the hints. Something strange has been going on with Jan all episode: the soppy Damien Rice soundtrack, the wistful dream sequences of the good times she shared with Harry. Her Harry. The one she fell for in the first place. She seems to have forgiven him. They’re even taking a family holiday.

But there’s one last stop before they go: Harrigan HQ, where Eddie is lying in wait. While Harry negotiates the terms of the holiday with Conrad, Gina and Jan stroll over to the lake. It’s Eddie’s moment, his chance to shine. He plants the Semtex in their car, hides around the corner, arms the bomb, finger hovering over the detonator … then a phone call.

By Finn McCool’s cobblers, it’s Kelly “Mad Dog” O’Connor, the lunatic son of the Medusa, whom Maeve sent Eddie to kill. “I’m coming for you,” growls the voice. “The Mad Dog is coming.”

Gina, Jan and Harry return to the car. Only Jan gets in. In his quivering, fearful state, Eddie trips and drops the detonator.

That’s Jan up in smoke, along with Harry’s plans for a quiet life. It may spell the end of Jan and all her problems, but it’s only the beginning of Eddie’s. He now has Mad Dog O’Connor to answer to, and Harry.

Then the credits roll, and what a gloriously cruel place to leave it.

The only other loose end is Frankie. We’re left to assume he met a long, painful death at the business end of Maeve’s car battery, but we never see a body. I truly hope he does a Harry and wriggles out of one last tight hole—Maeve’s hole—with one good knee and a long memory.

Season 3 can’t come soon enough. When Harry works out whose finger was on the detonator, Eddie’ll wish a knifey little twerp is all he could be.

Bolton Wanderers dodged a transfer bullet in walking away from £3m striker pursuit amid new blow: Opinion

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Deadline Day, both Bolton Wanderers and Preston North End were in the market for a striker. At the last hour, Celtic striker Johnny Kenny, who had spent the second half of the season prior at the Trotters, was made available.

Steven Schumacher‘s side would make a move for the 23-year-old, but Celtic’s price tag and PNE’s £3m offer would see BWFC walk away from a deal. And, Republic of Ireland international Kenny duly made the move to Deepdale.

Now though, according to The Lancashire Post’s Dan Murphy, Kenny is now out for the next couple of months with a hamstring injury. The striker’s injury struggles have meant that, in hindsight, Bolton Wanderers were better off looking elsewhere.

At the start of summer, Schumacher made it clear he’d welcome back a number of Bolton Wanderers loan stars, including Johnny Kenny. However, BWFC would not have the same spending power they had in League One, and being frugal with their budget would be at the top of the priority list.

The Trotters made 13 signings and Kenny would have been their marquee addition of the summer. The 23-year-old scored six goals, with a total of nine goal involvements in his time with the Trotters. In the end, the £3m price tag put Wanderers off, and they look right to have walked away.

Kenny has two goals so far for Preston North End but has played just eight games, having also suffered an injury that made him miss the play-offs with BWFC. The North West outfit would not have been able to bring in as many players if they had signed the forward, and so far they are nine points above their Lancashire rivals.

So far, Kenny has missed three games for the Lilywhites and could now miss at least the next 15 as he recovers from his new hamstring injury. The striker was one of Preston North End’s big signings, and with both them and Bolton Wanderers in a relegation fight, it is easy to see which team is in a better position after their summer recruitment.

For the Trotters, £3m is a lot of money, if not all their transfer budget, and it looks like they’ve dodged a serious problem by walking away from the deal.

Theo Bair, who signed the same day as Kenny joined PNE, has three goal involvements whilst Sam Dalby has six goals, sitting sixth in the Championship goalscoring charts. BWFC may still need more firepower at the top of the pitch, but they also need players on the pitch, and right now their double act is doing just that, whilst Kenny remains sidelined.
